Proof of "Slow Rate"
\chi^{2}
-DRO-REBEL

We first state the following dual reformulation for
\chi^{2}
-DRO.

Lemma 28

(Dual reformulation of
\chi^{2}
-DRO-REBEL)
.

Let
\ell(z;\theta)
be the REBEL loss. The
\chi^{2}
-DRO-REBEL objective admits the dual form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}\bigl(\theta;\varepsilon\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\bigl(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2}\bigr)}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\lambda\in[\underline{\lambda},\bar{\lambda}]}\Bigl\{\lambda\,\varepsilon\;+\;\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\;-\;2\lambda\;+\;\frac{1}{4\lambda}\,\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\bigl(\ell(z;\theta)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}[\,\ell(z;\theta)\,]+2\lambda\bigr)^{2}\bigr]\Bigr\}

where
0<\underline{\lambda}<\bar{\lambda}<\infty
are chosen so that the infimum is attained. Equivalently, defining
\mu=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}[\ell(z;\theta)]
and
\sigma^{2}=\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}(\ell(z;\theta))
,

\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}\bigl(\theta;\varepsilon\bigr)=\mu\;+\;\inf_{\lambda\in[\underline{\lambda},\bar{\lambda}]}\!\Bigl\{(\varepsilon-1)\,\lambda\;+\;\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4\lambda}\Bigr\}.

E.1
Proof of Strong Convexity of
\chi^{2}
-DRO-REBEL

We will again demonstrate strong convexity for
\chi^{2}
.

Lemma 29

(Strong convexity of
\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}
)
.

Let
l(z;\theta)
be the REBEL loss function. Then
\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}\left(\theta;\varepsilon\right)=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2}\right)}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]
is
2\lambda/\eta
-strongly convex with respect to Euclidean norm
||\cdot||_{2}
where
\lambda
is the regularity parameter from Assumption
3
.

Proof of Lemma
29
.

In Lemma
24
, we proved the strong convexity of
h
. By Lemma
8
, for
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
, this is equivalent to

h(\alpha\theta+(1-\alpha)\theta^{\prime};\mathbb{P})\leq\alpha h(\theta;\mathbb{P})+(1-\alpha)h(\theta^{\prime};\mathbb{P})-\frac{\mu}{2}\alpha(1-\alpha)||\theta-\theta^{\prime}||_{\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}}}^{2}.

Taking the supremum over
\mathbb{P}
preserves the convex combination and the negative quadratic term so we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}\left(\alpha\theta+(1-\alpha)\theta^{\prime};\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2}\right)}h(\alpha\theta+(1-\alpha)\theta^{\prime};\mathbb{P})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2}\right)}\left[\alpha h(\theta;\mathbb{P})+(1-\alpha)h(\theta^{\prime};\mathbb{P})-\frac{\mu}{2}\alpha(1-\alpha)||\theta-\theta^{\prime}||_{\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}\left(\theta;\varepsilon\right)+(1-\alpha)\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}\left(\theta^{\prime};\varepsilon\right)-\frac{\mu}{2}\alpha(1-\alpha)\inf_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2}\right)}||\theta-\theta^{\prime}||_{\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}\left(\theta;\varepsilon\right)+(1-\alpha)\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}\left(\theta^{\prime};\varepsilon\right)-\frac{\mu}{2}\alpha(1-\alpha)\inf_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2}\right)}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}\left(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}}\right)||\theta-\theta^{\prime}||_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}\left(\theta;\varepsilon\right)+(1-\alpha)\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}\left(\theta^{\prime};\varepsilon\right)-\frac{\mu\lambda}{2}\alpha(1-\alpha)||\theta-\theta^{\prime}||_{2}^{2}

where the second inequality holds from
\sup_{x}\left(f(x)+g(x)\right)\leq\sup_{x}f(x)+\sup_{x}g(x)
, the third inequality holds by the fact that
\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}}\succeq\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}\left(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}}\right)I
, and the last inequality holds from Assumption
3
. Thus we conclude that
\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}
is
\mu\lambda
-strongly convex in the
||\cdot||_{2}
norm.
∎

E.2
Proof of Slow Parameter Estimation Rate of
\chi^{2}
-DRO-REBEL

We now prove the "slow rate" estimation error of
\chi^{2}
-DRO-REBEL.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Let

\theta^{\chi^{2}}\in\arg\min_{\theta}\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta;\varepsilon)

and

\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\chi^{2}}\in\arg\min_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta;\varepsilon)
.
By the dual reformulation (Lemma
28
), for any fixed
\theta

\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta;\varepsilon)=\mu+\inf_{\lambda\in[\underline{\lambda},\bar{\lambda}]}\Bigl\{(\varepsilon-1)\,\lambda+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4\lambda}\Bigr\},

and similarly

\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta;\varepsilon)=\mu_{n}+\inf_{\lambda\in[\underline{\lambda},\bar{\lambda}]}\Bigl\{(\varepsilon-1)\,\lambda+\frac{\sigma_{n}^{2}}{4\lambda}\Bigr\},

where

\mu=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}[\,\ell(z;\theta)\,]
,

\mu_{n}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\circ}}[\,\ell(z;\theta)\,]
,

\sigma^{2}=\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}(\ell(z;\theta))
,
and
\sigma_{n}^{2}=\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\circ}}(\ell(z;\theta))
. Using the dual reformulation we have that

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta;\varepsilon)-\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta;\varepsilon)\bigr|

\displaystyle\;=\;\bigl|\mu-\mu_{n}\bigr|\;+\;\bigl|\inf_{\lambda}g(\lambda)-\inf_{\lambda}g_{n}(\lambda)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\bigl|\mu-\mu_{n}\bigr|\;+\;\sup_{\lambda\in[\underline{\lambda},\bar{\lambda}]}\bigl|g(\lambda)-g_{n}(\lambda)\bigr|,

where

g(\lambda)=(\varepsilon-1)\lambda+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4\lambda}

g_{n}(\lambda)=(\varepsilon-1)\lambda+\frac{\sigma_{n}^{2}}{4\lambda}.

The first inequality in the argument above follows from
\inf_{x}f(x)-\inf_{x}g(x)\leq\sup_{x}|f(x)-g(x)|
. From Lemma
22
, the loss function
\ell(z;\theta)
is bounded within
[0,K_{\ell}]
. We use Hoeffding’s inequality (Lemma
17
) to bound the deviations of
\mu_{n}
and
\sigma_{n}^{2}
from their population counterparts. For any
\epsilon_{1}>0
and
\epsilon_{2}>0
:

\mathbb{P}\bigl(|\mu-\mu_{n}|\geq\epsilon_{1}\bigr)\;\leq\;2\exp\!\Bigl(-\frac{2n\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{K_{\ell}^{2}}\Bigr),

and since
\ell(z;\theta)\in[0,K_{\ell}]
,
(\ell(z;\theta)-\mu)^{2}\in[0,K_{\ell}^{2}]
. Thus, the range for the squared terms is
K_{\ell}^{2}
.

\mathbb{P}\bigl(|\sigma^{2}-\sigma_{n}^{2}|\geq\epsilon_{2}\bigr)\;\leq\;2\exp\!\Bigl(-\frac{2n\epsilon_{2}^{2}}{K_{\ell}^{4}}\Bigr).

Let the desired total failure probability for these bounds be
\delta\in(0,1)
. By a union bound, we require that each individual probability bound is at most
\delta/2
. For
|\mu-\mu_{n}|
, setting

2\exp\!\Bigl(-\frac{2n\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{K_{\ell}^{2}}\Bigr)=\frac{\delta}{2}

yields

\epsilon_{1}=K_{\ell}\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{2n}}.

For
|\sigma^{2}-\sigma_{n}^{2}|
, setting

2\exp\!\Bigl(-\frac{2n\epsilon_{2}^{2}}{K_{\ell}^{4}}\Bigr)=\frac{\delta}{2}

yields

\epsilon_{2}=K_{\ell}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{2n}}.

Therefore, by a union bound, with probability at least
1-\delta
, both of the following bounds hold simultaneously:

|\mu-\mu_{n}|\leq K_{\ell}\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{2n}}

|\sigma^{2}-\sigma_{n}^{2}|\leq K_{\ell}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{2n}}.

Consequently, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we can bound the difference between
g(\lambda)
and
g_{n}(\lambda)
:

\sup_{\lambda\in[\underline{\lambda},\bar{\lambda}]}|g(\lambda)-g_{n}(\lambda)|=\sup_{\lambda\in[\underline{\lambda},\bar{\lambda}]}\left|\frac{\sigma^{2}-\sigma_{n}^{2}}{4\lambda}\right|=\frac{|\sigma^{2}-\sigma_{n}^{2}|}{4\,\underline{\lambda}}\;\leq\;\frac{K_{\ell}^{2}}{4\,\underline{\lambda}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{2n}},

Combining these bounds, with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\bigl|\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta;\varepsilon)-\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta;\varepsilon)\bigr|\;\leq\;K_{\ell}\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{2n}}+\frac{K_{\ell}^{2}}{4\,\underline{\lambda}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{2n}}=K_{\ell}\Bigl(1+\frac{K_{\ell}}{4\underline{\lambda}}\Bigr)\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{2n}}.

Now we analyze the statistical estimation error
\|\theta^{\chi^{2}}-\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\chi^{2}}\|_{2}^{2}
. Using the “three-term” decomposition (as in the Wasserstein and KL proof), we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\chi^{2}};\varepsilon)-\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta^{\chi^{2}};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\chi^{2}};\varepsilon)-\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\chi^{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\chi^{2}};\varepsilon)\right|+\left|\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta^{\chi^{2}};\varepsilon)-\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta^{\chi^{2}};\varepsilon)\right|

\displaystyle=K_{\ell}\Bigl(1+\frac{K_{\ell}}{4\underline{\lambda}}\Bigr)\sqrt{\frac{2\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

Finally, by the strong convexity of
\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}
(cf. Lemma
9
),

\frac{\lambda}{\eta^{2}}\,\bigl\|\theta^{\chi^{2}}-\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\chi^{2}}\bigr\|^{2}\;\leq\;\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta^{\chi^{2}};\varepsilon)-\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\chi^{2}};\varepsilon),

Thus with probability at least
1-\delta
, we conclude

\|\theta^{\chi^{2}}-\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\chi^{2}}\|^{2}\leq\frac{\eta^{2}K_{g}^{2}}{\lambda}\left(1+K_{g}^{2}/4\underline{\lambda}\right)\sqrt{\frac{2\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

∎

Appendix F
Proof of "Master Theorem" for Parametric
n^{-1/2}
rates

Proof of Theorem
4
.

The proof proceeds in three main parts. First, we establish a basic inequality that relates the true excess risk of the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}
to an empirical process term and the remainder
\Delta_{n}
. Second, we use a peeling argument combined with localized Rademacher complexity to uniformly bound this empirical process and subsequently a high-probability bound on the excess risk
\varepsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})
. Finally, we use the strong convexity assumption to convert this risk bound into the final bound on the parameter error
\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{\star}\|_{2}
.

Let us define our key quantities:

Empirical and Population Robust Loss Minimizers:

Let
\hat{\theta}_{n}=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathcal{L}^{D}_{n}\bigl(\theta;\varepsilon_{n}\bigr)
be the empirical robust loss minimizer and
\theta^{\star}=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathcal{L}^{D}\bigl(\theta;\varepsilon_{n}\bigr)
be the population robust loss minimizer.

Nominal Risks:

Let
\widetilde{R}(\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}[\ell(z;\theta)]
denote the population risk and
R_{n}(\theta):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(z_{i};\theta)
be the empirical risk.

Excess Risk:

We define the excess risk of a parameter
\theta
as
\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta):=R(\theta)-\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}R(\theta)
.

Centered Loss Class:

The class of centered loss functions is
\mathcal{F}:=\{f_{\theta}:z\mapsto\ell(z;\theta)-\ell(z;\theta^{\star})\mid\theta\in\Theta\}
.

Scaled Loss Class:

The scaled loss class is defined as
\mathcal{G}:=\{g_{\theta}(z)=4^{-k_{\theta}}f_{\theta}(z)\mid\theta\in\Theta\}
.

Empirical Process:

For a function
g
, we use the notation
(\mathbb{P}-\mathbb{P}_{n})g:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}[g(z)]-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(z_{i})
.

Shell Decomposition:

For any real value
r>0
, we define a shell index
k_{\theta}
for each
\theta\in\Theta
as

k_{\theta}=\inf\left\{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta)\leq r\cdot 4^{k}\right\}.

For the remainder of this proof, we will assume that
\varepsilon_{n}
is fixed and will denote
\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta)=\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta;\varepsilon_{n})
.

Part 1: The Basic Inequality

Let
\Delta_{n}(\theta):=\mathcal{L}_{n}^{D}(\theta)-R_{n}(\theta)
. Then we can decompose the excess risk as follows.

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})

\displaystyle=R(\hat{\theta}_{n})-\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}R(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{D}(\hat{\theta}_{n})-\Delta(\hat{\theta}_{n})-\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta^{*})+\Delta(\theta^{*})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{D}(\hat{\theta}_{n})-\mathcal{L}_{n}^{D}(\hat{\theta}_{n})+\mathcal{L}_{n}^{D}(\hat{\theta}_{n})-\mathcal{L}_{n}^{D}(\theta^{*})+\mathcal{L}_{n}^{D}(\theta^{*})-\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta^{*})+\Delta(\theta^{*})-\Delta(\hat{\theta}_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}^{D}(\hat{\theta}_{n})-\mathcal{L}_{n}^{D}(\hat{\theta}_{n})+\mathcal{L}_{n}^{D}(\theta^{*})-\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta^{*})+\Delta(\theta^{*})-\Delta(\hat{\theta}_{n})

\displaystyle=R(\hat{\theta}_{n})+\Delta(\hat{\theta}_{n})-R_{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n})-\Delta_{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n})+R_{n}(\theta^{*})+\Delta_{n}(\theta^{*})-R(\theta^{*})-\Delta(\theta^{*})+\Delta(\theta^{*})-\Delta(\hat{\theta}_{n})

\displaystyle=R(\hat{\theta}_{n})-R_{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n})+R_{n}(\theta^{*})-R(\theta^{*})+\Delta_{n}(\theta^{*})-\Delta_{n}(\hat{\theta}_{n})

\displaystyle\leq(\mathbb{P}-\mathbb{P}_{n})f_{\hat{\theta}_{n}}+2\Delta_{n}

\displaystyle=4^{k_{\theta}}(\mathbb{P}-\mathbb{P}_{n})g_{\hat{\theta}_{n}}+2\Delta_{n}

where the first inequality holds by definition of
\hat{\theta}_{n}
and the last inequality holds by assumption of the dual remainder bound. This inequality connects the quantity we want to bound,
\epsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})
, to the empirical process
(\mathbb{P}-\mathbb{P}_{n})f_{\hat{\theta}_{n}}
. Note that if
k_{\theta}=0
, then
\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta)\leq r
. Otherwise if
k_{\theta}\geq 1
, then by definition

r\cdot 4^{k_{\theta}-1}\leq\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta)\leq r\cdot 4^{k_{\theta}}\iff\frac{\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta)}{r}\leq 4^{k_{\theta}}\leq\frac{4\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta)}{r}.

For the sake of argument, assume that
(\mathbb{P}-\mathbb{P}_{n})g_{\hat{\theta}_{n}}\leq\Upsilon
. Then the basic inequality implies
\epsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})\leq 4^{k_{\theta}}\Upsilon+2\Delta_{n}
. Consider cases

•

if
k_{\theta}=0
, then
\epsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})\leq\Upsilon+2\Delta_{n}

•

if
k_{\theta}\geq 1
, then
\epsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})\leq\frac{4\epsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})}{r}\Upsilon+2\Delta_{n}

•

if
\Upsilon\leq\frac{r}{8}
, then
\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})\leq 2\Delta_{n}.

We can combine these into one uniform bound
\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})\leq 2\Delta_{n}+\Upsilon
. From this we see that it is sufficient to prove that
(\mathbb{P}-\mathbb{P}_{n})g_{\hat{\theta}_{n}}\leq\Upsilon
holds with probability
1-\delta
uniformly across all excess risk shells.

Part 2: Bounding The Empirical Process

Let us define a sequence of radii
r_{k}=4^{k}r_{0}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, where
r_{0}
is a baseline risk level to be determined later. We partition the parameter space
\Theta
into disjoint shells based on these radii:

\Theta_{k}:=\begin{cases}\{\theta\in\Theta\mid\varepsilon_{\ell}(\theta)\leq r_{0}\}&\text{if }k=0\\
\{\theta\in\Theta\mid r_{k-1}<\varepsilon_{\ell}(\theta)\leq r_{k}\}&\text{if }k\geq 1.\end{cases}

Corresponding to each parameter shell
\Theta_{k}
, we define a function class
\mathcal{G}_{k}=\{g_{\theta}\mid\theta\in\Theta_{k}\}
. For a fixed shell
\Theta_{k}
, we apply a standard uniform deviation bound based on the Rademacher complexity (Theorem
12
). With probability at least
1-\delta_{k}
, the following holds for all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{k}
:

(P-P_{n})g\leq 2\underbrace{\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{k})}_{\text{(a)}}+4\underbrace{\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{k}}\lVert g\rVert_{\infty}}_{{\text{(b)}}}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(1/\delta_{k})}{n}}

(2.1)

where

\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{k})=\mathbb{E}_{\sigma,Z}[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{k}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}g(Z_{i})]

is the empirical Rademacher complexity with
\sigma_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d}}}{{\sim}}\mathrm{Unif}\{-1,1\}
. We will now compute (a) and (b). Before doing this, we will derive a parameter estimate deviation bound that we will use in our subsequent analysis. By assumption of the dual remainder bound, we know
\left\lvert\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta)\;-R(\theta)\right\rvert\;\leq\;\Delta_{n}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle R(\theta)-\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}R(\theta^{*})

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta)-\Delta_{n}-\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}R(\theta)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta)-\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta^{*})-2\Delta_{n}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}\lVert\theta-\theta^{*}\rVert_{2}^{2}-2\Delta_{n}

where the last inequality holds from the assumption that
\mathcal{L}^{D}
is
\alpha
-strongly convex around
\theta^{*}
. Therefore, we deduce that

\displaystyle\lVert\theta-\theta^{*}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(R(\theta)-\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}R(\theta^{*})+2\Delta_{n}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta)+2\Delta_{n}\right)}.

(
\star
)

Computing (b):

Take
g\in\mathcal{G}
. Then, by definition,

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=4^{-k_{\theta}}\left\lvert\ell(z;\theta)-\ell(z;\theta^{*})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=4^{-k_{\theta}}\left\lvert\phi\left(h_{\theta}(z),z)\right)-\phi\left(h_{\theta^{*}}(z),z)\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 4^{-k_{\theta}}L_{\phi}\lVert h_{\theta}(z),z)-h_{\theta^{*}}(z),z)\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=4^{-k_{\theta}}L_{\phi}|\lVert v(z)\rVert_{2}\lVert\theta-\theta^{*}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4^{-k_{\theta}}L_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta)+2\Delta_{n}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 4^{-k_{\theta}}L_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(r\cdot 4^{k_{\theta}}+2\Delta_{n}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-k_{\theta}}L_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2r}{\alpha}}+4^{-k_{\theta}}L_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{4\Delta_{n}}{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq 2L_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{r+2\Delta_{n}}{\alpha}}

where the first inequality holds from
\ell(z;\theta)
being
L_{\phi}
-Lipschitz in
\theta
, the second holds from
h_{\theta}(z)
being a linear function of
v(z)
where
\lVert v(z)\rVert_{2}\leq 1
and (

⋆

‣
F
), the third holds by definition of
k_{\theta}
, the fourth from the fact that
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
, and the last from the fact that
2^{-k_{\theta}},4^{-k_{\theta}}\leq 1
and
\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}\leq\sqrt{2(a+b)}
.

Computing (a):

Next, we will use a "peeling" argument to get localization around the shells of
\mathcal{F}
. Define a countable sequence of function classes
\left(\mathcal{G}_{k}\right)_{k\geq 0}
such that
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\bigcup_{k}\mathcal{F}_{k}
. Define
\mathcal{F}_{k}
as follows

\mathcal{F}_{k}=\left\{4^{-k}f_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{k}\right\}.

Notice that across
\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
\epsilon_{l}(\theta)\leq r\cdot 4^{k_{\theta}}\leq r\cdot 4^{k}
. We now compute the Rademacher complexity of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n}\left(\mathcal{F}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4^{-k}\mathfrak{R}_{n}\left(\left\{f_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta,\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta)\leq r\cdot 4^{k}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4^{-k}\mathfrak{R}_{n}\left(\left\{z\mapsto\ell(z;\theta)-\ell(z;\theta^{*}):\theta\in\Theta,\lVert\theta-\theta^{*}\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(r\cdot 4^{k}+2\Delta_{n}\right)}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=4^{-k}\mathfrak{R}_{n}\left(\left\{z\mapsto\phi\left(h_{\theta}(z),z)\right)-\phi\left(h_{\theta^{*}}(z),z)\right):\theta\in\Theta,\lVert\theta-\theta^{*}\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(r\cdot 4^{k}+2\Delta_{n}\right)}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4^{-k}L_{\phi}\mathfrak{R}_{n}\left(\left\{z\mapsto\langle\theta-\theta^{*},z\rangle:\theta\in\Theta,\lVert\theta-\theta^{*}\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(r\cdot 4^{k}+2\Delta_{n}\right)}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4^{-k}L_{\phi}\mathfrak{R}_{n}\left(\left\{z\mapsto\langle\theta,z\rangle:\theta\in\Theta+\left\{\theta^{*}\right\},\lVert\theta\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(r\cdot 4^{k}+2\Delta_{n}\right)}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4^{-k}L_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha n}\left(r\cdot 4^{k}+2\Delta_{n}\right)}

where the second inequality holds definition of
g_{\theta}
coupled with the fact that
\left\{\theta\in\Theta:\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta)\leq r\cdot 4^{k}\right\}\subset\left\{\theta\in\Theta:\lVert\theta-\theta^{*}\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{2/\alpha\left(r\cdot 4^{k}+2\Delta_{n}\right)}\right\}
and the monotonicity of Rademacher complexity (Lemma
12
), the third inequality from
\phi
being the
L_{\phi}
Lipschitz and Ledoux-Talagrand contraction lemma (Lemma
11
), and the last inequality holds from a standard result on the Rademacher complexity of a linear predictor under the
\ell_{2}
metric (Lemma
14
).

Then by the monotonicity and subaddivity of the Rademacher complexity (Lemma
13
), we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{R}_{n}(\mathcal{F}_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}L_{\phi}}{\sqrt{\alpha n}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{r\cdot 4^{-k}+2\cdot 4^{-2k}\Delta_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}L_{\phi}}{\sqrt{\alpha n}}\left(\sqrt{r}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-k}+\sqrt{2\Delta_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}4^{-k}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}L_{\phi}}{\sqrt{\alpha n}}\left(2\sqrt{r}+\frac{4}{3}\sqrt{2\Delta_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sqrt{2}L_{\phi}}{\alpha n}\sqrt{r}+\frac{8L_{g}}{3\sqrt{\alpha n}}\sqrt{\Delta_{n}}

where the third inequality holds from
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
. Putting (a) and (b) together, we find that

\displaystyle\Upsilon

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\sqrt{2}L_{\phi}}{\sqrt{\alpha n}}\sqrt{r}+\frac{16L_{\phi}}{3\sqrt{\alpha n}}\sqrt{\Delta_{n}}+8L_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{r+2\Delta_{n}}{\alpha}}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{L_{\phi}}{\sqrt{\alpha n}}\left(4\sqrt{2}\sqrt{r}+\frac{16}{3}\sqrt{\Delta_{n}}+8\sqrt{2}\sqrt{(r+2\Delta_{n})\log(4/\delta)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{12L_{\phi}}{\sqrt{\alpha n}}\left(\sqrt{r+\Delta_{n}}+\sqrt{(r+\Delta_{n})\log(4/\delta)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{18L_{\phi}}{\sqrt{\alpha n}}\sqrt{\left(r+\Delta_{n}\right)\log(4e/\delta)}

where the second inequality holds by
\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}\leq\sqrt{2(a+b)}
and the upper bound of the constants by a common one, namely 12. The last inequality holds from
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
and is again upper bounded by a common constant. Now we shall try to satisfy
\Upsilon\leq r/8
or equivalently
8\Upsilon^{2}\leq r^{2}
. In our case, we take the following.

r\geq\frac{CL_{\phi}^{2}\log(4e/\delta)}{\alpha n}+\frac{\Delta_{n}}{2}

for a universal constant
C>0
1
1

1

Any universal constant
C\geq 4.2\times 10^{4}
will work.
. We can conclude that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})\leq 2\Delta_{n}+\frac{CL_{\phi}^{2}\log(4e/\delta)}{\alpha n}+\frac{\Delta_{n}}{2}.

Part 3: Converting From Excess Risk To Parameter Estimation

Now recall that from (

⋆

‣
F
),

\lVert\theta-\theta^{*}\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(\epsilon_{\ell}(\theta)+2\Delta_{n}\right)}.

Our high probability bound implies with probability at least
1-\delta

\varepsilon_{\ell}(\hat{\theta}_{n})\;\leq\;4\Delta_{n}+\frac{2C\,L_{\phi}^{2}\,\log(4e/\delta)}{\alpha n}.

Thus, we get the following.

\displaystyle\bigl\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{\star}\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\Bigl(4\Delta_{n}+\frac{2C\,L_{\phi}^{2}\,\log(4e/\delta)}{\alpha n}+2\Delta_{n}\Bigr)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha}\Bigl(6\Delta_{n}+\frac{2C\,L_{\phi}^{2}\,\log(4e/\delta)}{\alpha n}\Bigr)}.

Using
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
for
a,b\geq 0
yields

\bigl\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{\star}\bigr\|_{2}\;\leq\;\sqrt{\frac{12}{\alpha}}\;\sqrt{\Delta_{n}}\;+\;2\,\sqrt{\frac{C}{\alpha^{2}}}\;\frac{L_{\phi}}{\sqrt{n}}\,\sqrt{\log\!\frac{4e}{\delta}}.

Defining universal numerical constants

C_{1}=2\sqrt{C/\alpha},\;C_{2}=\sqrt{12/\alpha},

we can state:

\bigl\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{\star}\bigr\|_{2}\;\leq\;C_{1}\,\frac{L_{\phi}}{\alpha}\,\sqrt{\frac{\log(4e/\delta)}{\,n}}\;+\;C_{2}\,\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_{n}}{\alpha}}.

Assuming that the DRO approximation error
\Delta_{n}
(which reflects the inherent gap between the true and nominal risks in the ambiguity set) decays as
O(n^{-1})
, every term on the right-hand side is of order
O(n^{-1/2})
, which concludes the proof.

∎

Appendix G
Proof of Minimax Theorem for Robust RLHF Problems Under "Master" Theorem Assumptions

Proof of Theorem
5
.

The proof will proceed in two main parts. In the first part, we will establish that there is an irreducible statistical error term of order
\Omega(n^{-1/2})
by constructing the classic parameter separation construction and invoking Le Cam’s two-point lemma. In the second part, we establish an irreducible residual error (bias) term introduced by the DRO formulation of order
\Omega\left(\sqrt{\Delta_{n}/\alpha}\right)
. We will do this by constructing an example of loss function that satisfies the dual remainder bound assumption of the "Master" theorem and is strongly convex but whose minimizer is far from the true minimizer.

Let

\ell(z;\theta)=\phi\bigl(h_{\theta}(z),z\bigr),\qquad h_{\theta}(z)=\theta^{\!\top}\nu(z).

Define the
DRO population loss
and
Population Risk

\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta;\varepsilon_{n})\;=\;\sup_{P\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(P^{\circ};D)}\;\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P}\!\bigl[\ell(Z;\theta)\bigr],\qquad\mathcal{R}(\theta)\;=\;\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P^{\circ}}\!\bigl[\ell(Z;\theta)\bigr].

Throughout, we assume:

(A1) Local strong convexity.

There exist constants
\rho>0
and
\alpha>0
such that

\mathcal{L}^{D}(\cdot;\varepsilon_{n})
is
\alpha
–strongly convex on
the ball

B_{\rho}(\theta^{\star}):=\{\theta\in\Theta:\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|_{2}\leq\rho\},

where
\theta^{\star}\in\arg\min_{\theta}\mathcal{R}(\theta)
.

(A2) Linear margin.

\|v(z)\|_{2}\leq 1
for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.

(A3) Lipschitz loss in the margin.

|\phi(u,z)-\phi(u^{\prime},z)|\leq L_{\phi}\,|u-u^{\prime}|\quad\forall u,u^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R},\;z\in\mathcal{Z}.

(A4) Dual–remainder bound.

\bigl|\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta;\varepsilon_{n})-\mathcal{R}(\theta)\bigr|\leq\Delta_{n}\quad\forall\theta\in\Theta.

Fix
(\alpha,L_{\phi},\Delta_{n},\rho)
and set the admissible class as

\mathcal{M}(\alpha,L_{\phi},\Delta_{n},\rho):=\bigl\{(\mathbb{P}^{\circ},\phi,v)\;:\text{conditions {(A1)}\textendash{(A4)} hold}\bigr\}.

Part 1: Lower bound arising from statistical error

First notice that for any
\epsilon>0
, we have the following

\underbrace{\left\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Z}):\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D\right)\right\}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\subseteq\underbrace{\left\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Z}):\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D\right)\right\}}_{\mathcal{C}}.

Therefore,
\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{R}(\theta;\mathbb{P})\geq\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\mathcal{R}(\theta;\mathbb{P})
. Thus, it is sufficient to consider the non-robust case for obtaining the lower bound arising from statistical noise. Intuitively, since the DRO problem is more complex (for
\varepsilon>0
), it cannot possibly overcome this fundamental statistical limitation that comes from a simpler parametric problem. Now, since
\Delta_{n}
is a function of
\varepsilon_{n}
, we can assume that
\Delta_{n}=O(1)
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\Delta_{n}=0
. From the dual remainder assumption, this implies
\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta;\varepsilon_{n})=\mathcal{R}(\theta;\mathbb{P})
.

Consider
\hat{\theta}_{n}\in\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\theta;\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\circ})
. This is the standard problem in M-estimation where
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\theta;\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\circ})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(z_{i};\theta)
where
z_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d}}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{P}^{\circ}
. Let
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a fixed unit vector and
\delta_{n}>0
be some separation as a function of
n
. We will choose this in our analysis. We will now construct two hypotheses
H_{0},H_{1}

\displaystyle H_{0}:\theta_{0}=0\;,\;\;H_{1}:\theta_{1}=\delta_{n}u.

Notice that
\lVert\theta_{1}-\theta_{0}\rVert=\delta_{n}
. Consider the loss function
\ell(z;\theta)=\phi\bigl(h_{\theta}(z),z\bigr)
. By assumption, this function is
L_{\phi}
Lipschitz and the robust DRO loss (or in this case the risk) is
\alpha
-strongly convex.

To make these hypotheses concrete, we define a simple data-generating process that will follow a parametric linear form. This is to adhere to the Linear Margin assumption of the "Master" theorem. Let
z_{i}=(\nu_{i},y_{i})
be generated from the true model
y_{i}=\theta^{\top}\nu_{i}+\xi_{i}
where
\nu_{i}
are i.i.d random vectors with
\lVert\nu_{i}\rVert\leq 1
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\nu_{i}\nu_{i}^{\top}\right]\succeq\alpha\mathrm{I}_{d}
for some
\alpha>0
and
\xi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
where
\sigma^{2}=L_{\phi}/2\alpha
. One way to think of this concretely is that
\nu_{i}
is fundamentally a function of the prompt
x_{i}
and the action
a
as a feature map. Also note that from this data-generating distribution we have that conditional on
\nu_{i}

\displaystyle\text{under}\;H_{0}:y_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\text{under}\;H_{1}:y_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\delta_{n}u^{\top}\nu_{i},\sigma^{2}\right).

We show that these two hypotheses are hard to distinguish based on
n
samples. We will do this by bounding the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence between the joint distributions
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n},\mathbb{P}_{1}^{n}
.

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}^{n}||\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n}\right)

\displaystyle=nD_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}||\mathbb{P}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=n\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\delta_{n}u^{\top}\nu_{i},\sigma^{2}\right)||\;\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma^{2}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=n\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\frac{\left(\delta_{n}u^{\top}\nu\right)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{n\delta_{n}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}u^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{\nu}\left[\nu\nu^{\top}\right]u

\displaystyle=\frac{n\delta_{n}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}u^{\top}\Sigma u

where the first equality holds from the tensorization of the KL divergence (Lemma
19
) and the third equality holds from the calculation of the KL divergence of two univariate normal distributions (Lemma
20
). Choose
\delta_{n}=\sigma^{2}/(4nu^{\top}\Sigma u)
so
\delta_{n}=\Omega(n^{-1/2})
and
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}^{n}||\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n}\right)=1/8
. Now we can invoke Le Cam’s two-point lemma (Lemma
21
)

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\theta}_{n}}\sup_{\theta\in\left\{\theta_{0},\theta_{1}\right\}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\lVert\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta\rVert^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lVert\theta_{1}-\theta_{0}\rVert_{2}}{2}\left(1-d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathbb{P}_{1}^{n},\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lVert\theta_{1}-\theta_{0}\rVert_{2}}{2}\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}^{n}||\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{n}}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{4}\right)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{L_{\phi}}{\alpha}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}}\right)

where the second inequality holds from Pinsker’s inequality
d_{\mathrm{TV}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathrm{KL}}
(Theorem
14
) and the last equality holds from taking
\delta_{n}=\sigma^{2}/(4nu^{\top}\Sigma u)
and
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{1}^{n}||\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n}\right)=1/8
. Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{R}(\theta;\mathbb{P})

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\hat{\theta}_{n}}\sup_{\theta\in\left\{\theta_{0},\theta_{1}\right\}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\lVert\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta\rVert^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\Omega\left(\frac{L_{\phi}}{\alpha}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}}\right).

This completes the proof of the irreducible statistical error.

Part 2: Lower bound arising from residual error

The other bound arises from the inherent ambiguity introduced by the DRO formulation. First, let the data-generating distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\circ}
be the point mass on a single observation.

\displaystyle z_{0}=(\nu_{0},y_{0}),\;\nu_{0}:=e_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\;y_{0}=0.

By construction
\lVert\nu_{0}\rVert_{2}=1
. Define a globally
L_{\phi}
Lipschitz function but locally strongly convex scalar loss function
\psi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as the (scaled) Huberized quadratic:

\psi(u)=\begin{cases}\frac{\alpha}{2}u^{2}&\left\lvert u\right\rvert\leq r_{0}\\
\alpha r_{0}\left\lvert u\right\rvert-\frac{\alpha}{2}r_{0}^{2}&\left\lvert u\right\rvert>r_{0}\end{cases}

where
r_{0}:=\min\left\{L_{\phi}/\alpha,\rho\right\}
. Then notice that
\left\lvert\psi^{\prime}(u)\right\rvert\leq\alpha r_{0}\leq L_{\phi}
for all
u
and
\psi
is locally
\alpha
-strongly convex on
(-r_{0},r_{0})
. Set
\phi(u,z):=\psi(u)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\ell(z_{0};\theta)

\displaystyle=\psi(\theta^{\top}e_{1})

\displaystyle=\psi(\theta_{1}).

Note that
\psi(u)
attains a unique minimizer at
u=0
. Therefore, the (non-robust) population minimizer is
\theta^{*}=0
. Moreover, on the ball
\left\{\theta:\lVert\theta\rVert_{2}\leq r_{0}\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\rho}\left(\theta^{*}\right)
,
\mathcal{R}
is
\alpha
-strongly convex in
\theta
along the
e_{1}
directions. We will ensure that the DRO objective is
\alpha
-strongly convex on
\left\{\theta:\lVert\theta\rVert_{2}\leq r_{0}\right\}
.

Remark 6
.

We used the Huberized quadratic to ensure that (A3) and (A1) hold. Any
L_{\phi}
-Lipschitz
\psi
with
\psi^{\prime\prime}(0)\geq\alpha
would work.

Now choose

\delta:=\min\left\{\rho,\frac{r_{0}}{2},\sqrt{\frac{2\Delta_{n}}{\alpha}}\right\},\;\;\beta:=\frac{\alpha\delta}{2}.

We also define

g_{\pm}(\theta):=\pm\min\left\{\beta(\theta^{\top}e_{1}),\Delta_{n}\right\}.

Notice that for any
\theta\in\Theta
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert g_{\pm}(\theta)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\min\left\{\beta\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert,\Delta_{n}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\beta\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\beta\delta

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha\delta^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{n}

where the third inequality holds since
\left\{\theta:\lVert\theta\rVert_{2}\leq\delta\right\}\subseteq\left\{\theta:\lVert\theta\rVert_{2}\leq r_{0}\right\}
and the last inequality holds from the definition of
\delta
. Therefore, the dual remainder bound (A4) will hold for the perturbed objectives defined next.

Now we define the following admissible DRO objective,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\pm}(\theta):=\mathcal{R}(\theta)+g_{\pm}(\theta)=\psi(\theta_{1})\pm\min\left\{\beta(\theta^{\top}e_{1}),\Delta_{n}\right\}.

We will see why this DRO objective is admissible. On the slab
\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert\leq\delta
, we have
g_{\pm}(\theta)=\pm\beta\theta_{1}
which is a convex function. Hence, on the ball
\mathcal{B}_{\delta}\left(\theta^{*}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\rho}\left(\theta^{*}\right)\;\cap\;\left\{\left\lvert\theta_{1}\right\rvert\leq r_{0}\right\}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\pm}(\theta)=\psi(\theta_{1})\pm\beta\theta_{1}
is the sum of a strongly convex function and a convex function which must be strongly convex. Therefore,
\mathcal{L}^{\pm}
is
\alpha
-strongly convex on
\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(\theta^{*})
. Thus Assumption (A1) holds.

Now notice that both the plus and minus DRO objectives use the same base data-generating distribution
\mathbb{P^{\circ}}
; only the DRO objective is perturbed by
g_{\pm}
. Therefore, the sample
(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n})
is identical under both objectives, so
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{+}=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{-}
. Thus, the distributions are statistically indistinguishable, resulting in
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{+},\mathcal{P}_{n}^{-})=0
.

We will restrict ourselves to the line
\theta=\xi\theta_{1}
with
\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\leq\delta
(which contains both minimizers by strong convexity and symmetry). For
\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\leq\delta
,

\mathcal{L}^{\pm}(\xi e_{1})=\psi(\xi)\pm\beta\xi,\;\nabla_{\xi}\mathcal{L}^{\pm}(\xi e_{1})=\psi^{\prime}(\xi)\pm\beta.

Inside
\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\leq\delta\subseteq(-r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)
. we have
\psi^{\prime}(\xi)=\alpha\xi
. Setting the gradient to 0 and solving to
\xi
, we find that

\theta_{+}^{*}=\frac{\beta}{\alpha}e_{1}=\frac{\delta}{2}e_{1},\;\;\theta_{+}^{*}=-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}e_{1}=-\frac{\delta}{2}e_{1}.

Both lie inside
\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\leq\delta
and have separation

\lVert\theta_{+}^{*}-\theta_{-}^{*}\rVert=\delta\geq\sqrt{\frac{2\Delta_{n}}{\alpha}}\bm{1}_{\left\{\sqrt{2\Delta_{n}/\alpha}\leq\min\left\{\rho,r_{0}/2\right\}\right\}}.

Since
\theta_{+}^{*},\theta_{-}^{*}
belong to
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{+},\mathcal{P}_{n}^{-}
which are statistically indistinguishable, by Le Cam’s two-point lemma, we have for
every
estimator

\widehat{\theta}_{n}
,

\max\Bigl\{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{P}_{n}^{(+)}}\!\Bigl(\|\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{\star}_{+}\|_{2}\geq\delta/2\Bigr),\;\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{P}_{n}^{(-)}}\!\Bigl(\|\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{\star}_{-}\|_{2}\geq\delta/2\Bigr)\Bigr\}\;\;\geq\;\;\frac{1}{2}.

Then by Markov’s inequality

\sup_{j\in\{+,-\}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{n}^{(j)}}\!\bigl[\,\|\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{\star}_{j}\|_{2}\bigr]\;\geq\;\frac{\delta}{4}\geq c\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_{n}}{\alpha}}

where
c=\sqrt{2}/4
whenever
\sqrt{2\Delta_{n}/\alpha}\leq\min\{\rho,r_{0}/2\}
. Since
\Delta_{n}
is usually a function of
\rho
, this is not too restrictive.
If instead
\Delta_{n}
is larger, then
\delta\geq c^{\prime}\rho
for a universal
c^{\prime}>0
. Whether this is larger or smaller than the statistical error depends on how
\rho
changes with
n
, but the presence of the cap does not change the overall rate because the upper bound will contain the maximum of the statistical error and the residual error.

The total minimax error is lower-bounded by the maximum of the two error sources, as an estimator must be robust to both statistical noise and modeling bias. Therefore, combining the two parts, the overall minimax lower bound is:

\inf_{\tilde{\theta}_{n}}\sup_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}\in\mathcal{M}(\alpha,L_{\phi},\Delta_{n})}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\!\bigl[\,\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{\star}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ})\|_{2}\bigr]\geq c_{1}\frac{L_{\phi}}{\alpha}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}}\vee c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_{n}}{\alpha}}.

∎

Appendix H
Proofs of "Fast Rate" DRO-REBEL

H.1
Proof of "Fast Rate" Wasserstein-DRO-REBEL

Before we prove the necessary results to get the "fast rate" for Wasserstein-DRO-REBEL, we need to make an assumption on the loss functions
\ell(\cdot;\theta)
,
\theta\in\Theta
. Note that this assumption is only used in proving the dual "remainder" term holds.

Assumption 5
.

For the Wasserstein-DRO-REBEL objective, we assume that the pointwise loss function
\ell(z;\theta)
is
L_{\ell,z}
-Lipschitz with respect to its data argument
z=(x,a^{1},a^{2})
for all
\theta\in\Theta
. That is, there exists a constant
L_{\ell,z}\geq 0
such that for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}
,

\left\lvert\ell(z_{1};\theta)-\ell(z_{2};\theta)\right\rvert\leq L_{\ell,z}d(z_{1},z_{2})

where
d(\cdot,\cdot)
is the metric corresponding to the type-
p
Wasserstein distance used to define the ambiguity set.

Proof of Corollary
1
.

First, recall the type-p Wasserstein distance. The type-p (
p\in[1,\infty)
) Wasserstein distance between two distributions
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{M}\left(\Xi\right)
is defined as

\mathcal{W}_{p}\left(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\right)=\left(\inf_{\pi\in\Pi\left(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\right)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}d(\xi,\eta)^{p}\pi(d\xi,d\eta)\right)^{1/p}

where
\pi
is a coupling between the marginal distributions
\xi\sim\mathbb{P}
and
\eta\sim\mathbb{Q}
, and
d
is a pseudometric defined on
\mathcal{Z}
.

Let us bound the difference between expectations under
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\circ}
. For any coupling
\pi
between
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\circ}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]

\displaystyle=\int\ell(\xi;\theta)\pi(d\xi,d\eta)-\int\ell(\eta;\theta)\pi(d\xi,d\eta)

\displaystyle=\int\left(\ell(\xi;\theta)-\ell(\eta;\theta)\right)\pi(d\xi,d\eta).

This equality holds due to the marginal properties of a coupling. Now, assume that the loss function
\ell(z;\theta)
is
L_{\ell,z}
-Lipschitz with respect to
z
(that is,
\left\lvert\ell(\xi;\theta)-\ell(\eta;\theta)\right\rvert\leq L_{\ell,z}d(\xi,\eta)
for some constant
L_{\ell,z}
). Then, for
p\geq 1
, we can use Lemma
6
(monotonicity of the Wasserstein distance) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int\left\lvert\ell(\xi;\theta)-\ell(\eta;\theta)\right\rvert\pi(d\xi,d\eta)

\displaystyle\leq L_{\ell,z}\int d(\xi,\eta)\pi(d\xi,d\eta)

\displaystyle\leq L_{\ell,z}\left(\int d(\xi,\eta)^{p}\pi(d\xi,d\eta)\right)^{1/p}.

Taking the supremum over all couplings
\pi
, and then over all
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\mathcal{W}_{p})
:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{W}_{p}}(\theta)\;-\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\mathcal{W}_{p}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq L_{\ell,z}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\mathcal{W}_{p}\right)}\mathcal{W}_{p}\left(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\circ}\right)

\displaystyle\leq L_{\ell,z}\varepsilon_{n}.

Thus, we can set
\Delta_{n}=L_{\ell,z}\varepsilon_{n}
. If we choose
\varepsilon_{n}\asymp n^{-1}
, then
\Delta_{n}=O(n^{-1})
, which aligns with the condition for the
O(n^{-1/2})
rate in the Master Theorem.

We now show both DPO and REBEL satisfy Assumption
5
by deriving their respective Lipschitz constants with respect to the defined pseudometric. For any
z=(x,a^{1},a^{2},y)\in\mathcal{Z}
, we denote

\Delta\psi(z):=\psi(x,a^{1})-\psi(x,a^{2}),\quad\Delta r(z):=\phi(x,a^{1})^{\!\top}\omega-\phi(x,a^{2})^{\!\top}\omega,\quad h_{\theta}(z):=\theta^{\!\top}\Delta\psi(z).

We equip
\mathcal{Z}
with the pseudo–metric

d(z,z^{\prime})=\|\Delta\psi(z)-\Delta\psi(z^{\prime})\|_{2}+|\Delta r(z)-\Delta r(z^{\prime})|+|y-y^{\prime}|.

(i) DPO loss.

Define

f(h,y):=-y\log\sigma(\beta h)-(1-y)\log\sigma(-\beta h)

so that
\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}(z;\theta)=f\bigl(h_{\theta}(z),y\bigr)
. We recall the following bounds from Assumptions
1
and
2
:
\|\theta\|_{2}\leq B
and
\|\psi(x,a)\|_{2}\leq 1
. These imply

\|\Delta\psi(z)\|_{2}\leq\|\psi(x,a^{1})\|_{2}+\|\psi(x,a^{2})\|_{2}\leq 2.

Consequently,

|h_{\theta}(z)|\leq\|\theta\|_{2}\|\Delta\psi(z)\|_{2}\leq 2B.

We compute the partial derivatives of
f(h,y)
:

\frac{\partial f}{\partial h}=-y\frac{d}{dh}(\log\sigma(\beta h))-(1-y)\frac{d}{dh}(\log\sigma(-\beta h)).

Using
\frac{d}{du}(\log\sigma(u))=1-\sigma(u)=\sigma(-u)
:

\frac{\partial f}{\partial h}=-y(\beta(1-\sigma(\beta h)))-(1-y)(-\beta(1-\sigma(-\beta h)))=\beta((1-y)\sigma(\beta h)-y\sigma(-\beta h)).

For
y=1
,
|\partial f/\partial h|=|-\beta\sigma(-\beta h)|<\beta
since
0<\sigma(u)<1
. For
y=0
,
|\partial f/\partial h|=|\beta\sigma(\beta h)|<\beta
. Thus,

\bigl|\partial f/\partial h\bigr|\leq\beta.

For the partial derivative with respect to
y
:

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial f/\partial y\bigr|=|-\log\sigma(\beta h)+\log\sigma(-\beta h)|

\displaystyle=\left|\log\left(\frac{\sigma(-\beta h)}{\sigma(\beta h)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\log\left(\frac{1/(1+e^{\beta h})}{1/(1+e^{-\beta h})}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\log\left(\frac{1+e^{-\beta h}}{1+e^{\beta h}}\right)\right|.

The maximum value of
|\log(\frac{1+e^{-u}}{1+e^{u}})|
for
u\in[-C,C]
is
C
. Since
|h|\leq 2B
, we have
|\beta h|\leq 2\beta B
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial f/\partial y\bigr|\leq 2\beta B.

Since

|\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}(z;\theta)-\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}(z^{\prime};\theta)|\leq|\partial f/\partial h||h_{\theta}(z)-h_{\theta}(z^{\prime})|+|\partial f/\partial y||y-y^{\prime}|

by the mean-value inequality, and

|h_{\theta}(z)-h_{\theta}(z^{\prime})|\leq B\,\|\Delta\psi(z)-\Delta\psi(z^{\prime})\|_{2}

we have:

\displaystyle|\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}(z;\theta)-\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}(z^{\prime};\theta)|

\displaystyle\leq\beta B\,\|\Delta\psi(z)-\Delta\psi(z^{\prime})\|_{2}+2\beta B\,|y-y^{\prime}|.

The DPO loss does not depend on
\Delta r(z)
. Given the metric
d
, the Lipschitz constant
L_{\ell,z}^{\mathrm{DPO}}
is the sum of the coefficients corresponding to the terms in
d(z,z^{\prime})
that affect the loss.
Thus,

L_{\ell,z}^{\mathrm{DPO}}=\beta B+2\beta B=3\beta B.

(ii) REBEL loss.

Set
g(h,\Delta r):=[\eta^{-1}(h-\Delta r)]^{2}
so that
\ell_{\mathrm{REBEL}}(z;\theta)=g\bigl(h_{\theta}(z),\Delta r(z)\bigr)
. From Assumptions
2
and
1
, we have
|h_{\theta}(z)|\leq 2B
and
|\Delta r(z)|\leq 2F
. Thus,

\displaystyle|h-\Delta r|

\displaystyle\leq|h|+|\Delta r|

\displaystyle=2(B+F).

The partial derivatives of
g(h,\Delta r)
satisfy:

\displaystyle|\partial g/\partial h|=|\partial g/\partial\,\Delta r|

\displaystyle=|2\eta^{-2}(h-\Delta r)|

\displaystyle\leq 4\eta^{-2}(B+F).

By the mean-value inequality,

|\ell_{\mathrm{REBEL}}(z;\theta)-\ell_{\mathrm{REBEL}}(z^{\prime};\theta)|\leq|\partial g/\partial h||h_{\theta}(z)-h_{\theta}(z^{\prime})|+|\partial g/\partial\Delta r||\Delta r(z)-\Delta r(z^{\prime})|.

Using

|h_{\theta}(z)-h_{\theta}(z^{\prime})|\leq B\,\|\Delta\psi(z)-\Delta\psi(z^{\prime})\|_{2}

and the partial derivative bounds, it follows that

|\ell_{\mathrm{REBEL}}(z;\theta)-\ell_{\mathrm{REBEL}}(z^{\prime};\theta)|\leq 4\eta^{-2}(B+F)B\|\Delta\psi(z)-\Delta\psi(z^{\prime})\|_{2}+4\eta^{-2}(B+F)|\Delta r(z)-\Delta r(z^{\prime})|.

The REBEL loss does not depend on
y
. Given the metric
d
, the Lipschitz constant
L_{\ell,z}^{\mathrm{REBEL}}
is the sum of the coefficients corresponding to the terms in
d(z,z^{\prime})
that affect the loss,

L_{\ell,z}^{\mathrm{REBEL}}=4\eta^{-2}(B+F)B+4\eta^{-2}(B+F)=4\eta^{-2}(B+F)(B+1).

Hence, both DPO and REBEL satisfy Assumption
5
with
L_{\ell,z}^{\mathrm{DPO}}=3\beta B
and
L_{\ell,z}^{\mathrm{REBEL}}=4\eta^{-2}(B+F)(B+1)
.
∎

H.2
Proof of "Fast Rate" KL-DRO-REBEL

Proof of Corollary
2
.

First, recall Lemma
15
(Gibbs variational principle characterization of the KL divergence) for probability measures
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
:

D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{Q}\right)=\sup_{g:\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[g\right]-\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[e^{g}\right]\right\},

Let
f=\ell(z;\theta)
. For any
\lambda\geq 0
, we can choose
g(z)=\lambda(f(z)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f])
to obtain a lower bound for
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{Q})
:

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{Q}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{\lambda\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f]\right)-\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[e^{\lambda\left(f-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f]\right)}\right]\right\},

Now, suppose that
f=\ell(z;\theta)\in[0,K_{\ell}]
almost surely (from Lemma
22
). By Hoeffding’s Lemma (Lemma
16
), if
f
is bounded in
[0,K_{\ell}]
, then
f-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f]
is sub-Gaussian with parameter
K_{\ell}/2
. Thus, we have that

\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[e^{\lambda\left(f-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f]\right)}\right]\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}(K_{\ell}/2)^{2}}{2}=\frac{\lambda^{2}K_{\ell}^{2}}{8}.

Substituting this bound into the KL inequality:

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{Q}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\lambda\geq 0}\left\{\lambda\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f]\right)-\frac{\lambda^{2}K_{\ell}^{2}}{8}\right\}.

The expression in the curly brackets is a concave quadratic in
\lambda
. Its supremum is attained at

\lambda^{*}=\frac{4\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f]\right)}{K_{\ell}^{2}}.

Plugging this optimal
\lambda^{*}
back into the expression, we find that

D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{Q}\right)\geq\frac{2\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f]\right)^{2}}{K_{\ell}^{2}}.

Rearranging terms, we obtain a bound on the absolute difference in expectations:

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[f]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f]\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}K_{\ell}\sqrt{D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{Q}\right)}.

Now, taking
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{P}^{\circ}
and
f=\ell(z;\theta)
, and considering the supremum within the KL ball
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\mathrm{KL})
:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{KL}}(\theta)\;-\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\mathrm{KL}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}K_{\ell}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\mathrm{KL}\right)}\sqrt{D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{P}^{\circ}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}K_{\ell}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}.

Thus, we can set
\Delta_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}K_{\ell}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}
. If we choose
\varepsilon_{n}\asymp n^{-2}
, then
\Delta_{n}=O(n^{-1})
, which satisfies the condition for the
O(n^{-1/2})
rate in the Master Theorem.
∎

H.3
Proof of "Fast Rate"
\chi^{2}
-DRO-REBEL

Proof of Corollary
3
.

By Theorem
13
(Hammersley-Chapman-Robbins (HCR)
lower bound), we immediately have:

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]\right\rvert\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\left(\ell(z;\theta)\right)\chi^{2}\left(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{P}^{\circ}\right)}.

Since
\ell(z;\theta)\in[0,K_{\ell}]
almost surely, by Lemma
18
, its variance is bounded by
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\left(\ell(z;\theta)\right)\leq K_{\ell}^{2}/4
. Thus, we have:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}(\theta)\;-\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2}\right)}\sqrt{\frac{K_{\ell}^{2}}{4}\chi^{2}\left(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{P}^{\circ}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2}\right)}\sqrt{\chi^{2}\left(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{P}^{\circ}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}.

Thus, we can set
\Delta_{n}=\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}
. If we choose
\varepsilon_{n}\asymp n^{-2}
, then
\Delta_{n}=O(n^{-1})
, which satisfies the condition for the
O(n^{-1/2})
rate in the Master Theorem.
∎

H.4
Proof of "Fast Rate" for General f-Divergences

Before we prove the necessary results to get the "fast rate" for general f-divergences, we need to make an assumption on the loss function
\ell(\cdot;\theta)
,
\theta\in\Theta
and the form of the f-divergence. Note that this assumption is only used in proving that the dual "remainder" term holds:

Assumption 6
.

The pointwise loss function
\ell(z;\theta)
is bounded for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
\theta\in\Theta
. That is, there exist constants
m,M\in\mathbb{R}
such that
m\leq\ell(z;\theta)\leq M
almost surely with respect to any relevant probability measure. Let
K_{\ell}=M-m
be the range of the loss function.

Assumption 7
.

Let
f:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
be a convex function satisfying
f(1)=0
. Furthermore, we assume:

1.

f
is twice continuously differentiable at
t=1
.

2.

f^{\prime\prime}(1)>0
. This implies that
f
is strictly convex at
t=1
.

Proof of Theorem
6
.

Let
f
be a convex function satisfying Assumption
7
. The f-divergence
D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})
has the variational representation:

D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[g]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f^{*}(g)]\right\}

where
f^{*}
is the Fenchel conjugate of
f
, defined as
f^{*}(y)=\sup_{x\in[0,\infty)}\{xy-f(x)\}
, and
\mathcal{G}
is the set of all measurable functions for which the expectations are finite.

We are interested in bounding
\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\ell(z;\theta)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\ell(z;\theta)]\right\rvert
. Let
h(z)=\ell(z;\theta)
.
By Assumption
6
,
h(z)\in[m,M]
almost surely.
Let
\Delta_{h}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h]
.

Since
f
is convex and twice continuously differentiable at
1
with
f(1)=0
and
f^{\prime\prime}(1)>0
, its Fenchel conjugate
f^{*}
is convex and twice continuously differentiable at
0
. Furthermore, we have:

•

f^{*}(0)=-\min_{x}f(x)
. If
f
is minimized at
x=1
(which is typically the case for f-divergences), and
f(1)=0
, then
f^{*}(0)=0
.

•

(f^{*})^{\prime}(0)=\arg\min_{x}f(x)
. Under the same conditions,
(f^{*})^{\prime}(0)=1
.

•

(f^{*})^{\prime\prime}(0)=1/f^{\prime\prime}(1)
. Let
C_{f}=\frac{1}{f^{\prime\prime}(1)}
. Since
f^{\prime\prime}(1)>0
,
C_{f}
is a finite positive constant.

By Taylor’s theorem with a remainder term, for any
y
in a neighborhood of
0
, we can write:

f^{*}(y)=f^{*}(0)+(f^{*})^{\prime}(0)y+\frac{1}{2}(f^{*})^{\prime\prime}(0)y^{2}+R_{2}(y)

where
R_{2}(y)
is the remainder term, satisfying
\lim_{y\to 0}R_{2}(y)/y^{2}=0
.
Given the properties above, this becomes:

f^{*}(y)=y+\frac{1}{2}C_{f}y^{2}+R_{2}(y).

Choose
g(z)=\lambda(h(z)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h])
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[g]=0
.
The variational representation gives us the result that

D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})\geq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[g]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[f^{*}(g)].

Substitute the Taylor expansion of
f^{*}(g(z))
into the inequality:

D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})\geq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\lambda(h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h])]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\lambda(h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h])+\frac{1}{2}C_{f}\lambda^{2}(h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h])^{2}+R_{2}(\lambda(h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h]))\right]

D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})\geq\lambda(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h])-\lambda\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h]]-\frac{1}{2}C_{f}\lambda^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[(h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h])^{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[R_{2}(\lambda(h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h]))].

Since
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h]]=0
, we have:

D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})\geq\lambda\Delta_{h}-\frac{1}{2}C_{f}\lambda^{2}\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{Q}}(h)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[R_{2}(\lambda(h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h]))].

By Assumption
6
,
h(z)\in[m,M]
. Thus,
(h(z)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h])\in[m-M,M-m]=[-K_{\ell},K_{\ell}]
.
Therefore,
\left\lvert h(z)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h]\right\rvert\leq K_{\ell}
.
By Popoviciu’s inequality (Lemma
18
),
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}(h)\leq K_{\ell}^{2}/4
.

Let
Y=h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h]
. Then
\left\lvert Y\right\rvert\leq K_{\ell}
.
The remainder term
R_{2}(y)
satisfies
\left\lvert R_{2}(y)\right\rvert\leq\epsilon(y)y^{2}
for some function
\epsilon(y)
such that
\lim_{y\to 0}\epsilon(y)=0
.
Thus, for any
\delta_{0}>0
, there exists
\Lambda>0
such that for
\left\lvert y\right\rvert\leq\Lambda
,
\left\lvert\epsilon(y)\right\rvert\leq\delta_{0}
.

If we choose
\lambda
so that
\left\lvert\lambda\right\rvert K_{\ell}\leq\Lambda
, then
\left\lvert\lambda Y\right\rvert\leq\Lambda
.
So,
\left\lvert R_{2}(\lambda Y)\right\rvert\leq\delta_{0}(\lambda Y)^{2}
.
Then,
\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[R_{2}(\lambda Y)]\right\rvert\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\delta_{0}(\lambda Y)^{2}]=\delta_{0}\lambda^{2}\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{Q}}(h)
.

Combining these, we get the following

D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})\geq\lambda\Delta_{h}-\frac{1}{2}C_{f}\lambda^{2}\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{Q}}(h)-\delta_{0}\lambda^{2}\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{Q}}(h)

D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})\geq\lambda\Delta_{h}-\left(\frac{C_{f}}{2}+\delta_{0}\right)\lambda^{2}\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{Q}}(h).

Using
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}(h)\leq K_{\ell}^{2}/4
,

D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})\geq\lambda\Delta_{h}-\left(\frac{C_{f}}{2}+\delta_{0}\right)\lambda^{2}\frac{K_{\ell}^{2}}{4}.

Let
B_{\delta_{0}}=K_{\ell}^{2}\left(C_{f}/2+\delta_{0}\right)/4
. The right-hand side is a concave quadratic in
\lambda
:
A\lambda-B_{\delta_{0}}\lambda^{2}
, with
A=\Delta_{h}
.
This quadratic is maximized at

\lambda^{*}=\frac{A}{2B_{\delta_{0}}}=\frac{\Delta_{h}}{2\left(\frac{C_{f}}{2}+\delta_{0}\right)\frac{K_{\ell}^{2}}{4}}=\frac{2\Delta_{h}}{\left(C_{f}+2\delta_{0}\right)\frac{K_{\ell}^{2}}{2}}.

Plugging
\lambda^{*}
back into the inequality

\displaystyle D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\geq\lambda^{*}\Delta_{h}-B_{\delta_{0}}(\lambda^{*})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\Delta_{h})^{2}}{2B_{\delta_{0}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\Delta_{h})^{2}}{2\left(\frac{C_{f}}{2}+\delta_{0}\right)\frac{K_{\ell}^{2}}{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\Delta_{h})^{2}}{\left(C_{f}+2\delta_{0}\right)\frac{K_{\ell}^{2}}{2}}.

Rearranging to bound
\left\lvert\Delta_{h}\right\rvert

\left\lvert\Delta_{h}\right\rvert^{2}\leq\frac{\left(C_{f}+2\delta_{0}\right)K_{\ell}^{2}}{2}D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\ell(z;\theta)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\ell(z;\theta)]\right\rvert\leq\frac{K_{\ell}\sqrt{C_{f}+2\delta_{0}}}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{Q})}.

The choice of
\delta_{0}
(and thus
\Lambda
) depends on how small
\left\lvert\lambda^{*}K_{\ell}\right\rvert
needs to be.
Since

\left\lvert\lambda^{*}K_{\ell}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\frac{2\Delta_{h}}{\left(C_{f}+2\delta_{0}\right)K_{\ell}^{2}}\right\rvert K_{\ell}=\frac{2\left\lvert\Delta_{h}\right\rvert}{\left(C_{f}+2\delta_{0}\right)K_{\ell}}.

the condition
\left\lvert\lambda^{*}K_{\ell}\right\rvert\leq\Lambda
becomes,

\left\lvert\Delta_{h}\right\rvert\leq\frac{\left(C_{f}+2\delta_{0}\right)K_{\ell}\Lambda}{2},

As we consider the supremum over
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D_{f})
,
D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{P}^{\circ})\leq\varepsilon_{n}
.
As
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
,
D_{f}(\mathbb{P}||\mathbb{P}^{\circ})\to 0
. By the derived inequality, this implies
\left\lvert\Delta_{h}\right\rvert\to 0
.
Therefore, for any given
\epsilon>0
, we can choose
\varepsilon_{n}
small enough such that
\left\lvert\Delta_{h}\right\rvert
is sufficiently small. This allows us to pick
\delta_{0}
small enough (e.g.
\delta_{0}<\epsilon
) to satisfy the condition on
\left\lvert\lambda Y\right\rvert\leq\Lambda
.
Thus, for
\varepsilon_{n}
sufficiently small,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{L}^{f}(\theta)\;-\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D_{f}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{\ell}\sqrt{C_{f}+2\delta_{0}}}{\sqrt{2}}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D_{f}\right)}\sqrt{D_{f}\left(\mathbb{P}\mid\mid\mathbb{P}^{\circ}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{\ell}\sqrt{C_{f}+2\delta_{0}}}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}.

Since
\delta_{0}
can be made arbitrarily small as
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
, this leads to

\left\lvert\mathcal{L}^{f}(\theta)\;-\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert\lesssim\frac{K_{\ell}\sqrt{C_{f}}}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}.

Thus, we can set
\Delta_{n}=\frac{K_{\ell}\sqrt{C_{f}}}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{n}}
. If we choose
\varepsilon_{n}\asymp n^{-2}
, then
\Delta_{n}=O(n^{-1})
, which satisfies the condition for the
O(n^{-1/2})
rate in the Master Theorem.
∎

H.5
Proof of "Fast Rate" for Total Variation Distance

Proof.

The Total Variation (TV) distance between two probability measures
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
is defined as:

D_{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=\sup_{h:\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\|h\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h(Z)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h(Z)]\right\rvert

where
\|h\|_{\infty}=\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left\lvert h(z)\right\rvert
is the supremum norm. Let
h_{0}(z)=\ell(z;\theta)
be the pointwise loss function. By Theorem
22
, we have
0\leq h_{0}(z)\leq K_{\ell}
almost surely. Consider the difference in expected loss under
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
:

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h_{0}(Z)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h_{0}(Z)]\right\rvert.

To relate this difference to the TV distance, we construct a function
h(z)
that satisfies the condition
\|h\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.
Since
h_{0}(z)\in[0,K_{\ell}]
, the midpoint of this interval is
K_{\ell}/2
, and the range is
K_{\ell}
.
Define
h(z)
as a scaled and shifted version of
h_{0}(z)
,

h(z)=\frac{h_{0}(z)-\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}}{\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}}=\frac{2h_{0}(z)-K_{\ell}}{K_{\ell}}.

Let’s verify the bounds on
h(z)
:

-\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}\leq h_{0}(z)-\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}\leq\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}.

Dividing by
\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}
(which is positive, as
K_{\ell}>0
by Theorem
22
):

-1\leq\frac{h_{0}(z)-\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}}{\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}}\leq 1.

Thus,
\left\lvert h(z)\right\rvert\leq 1
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, meaning
\|h\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.

Now, let’s express
h_{0}(z)
in terms of
h(z)
:

h_{0}(z)=\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}h(z)+\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}=\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}(h(z)+1).

Substitute this into the difference of expectations:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h_{0}(Z)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h_{0}(Z)]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}(h(Z)+1)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}(h(Z)+1)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h(Z)]+1)-\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h(Z)]+1)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h(Z)]-\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h(Z)]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h(Z)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h(Z)]\right\rvert.

Since
\|h\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, from the definition of Total Variation distance, we know that
\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h(Z)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h(Z)]\right\rvert\leq D_{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
.
Therefore, we can write

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\ell(z;\theta)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\ell(z;\theta)]\right\rvert\leq\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}D_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})

This inequality holds for any pair of probability measures
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
.

Now, we consider the definition of the robust loss function
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{TV}}(\theta)
over a TV-ball of radius
\varepsilon_{n}
:

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{TV}}(\theta)=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D_{\mathrm{TV}}\right)}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]

where
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D_{\mathrm{TV}}\right)=\{\mathbb{P}\mid D_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\circ})\leq\varepsilon_{n}\}
.

We want to bound
\left\lvert\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{TV}}(\theta)\;-\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert
.
Since
\mathbb{P}^{\circ}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D_{\mathrm{TV}})
(as
D_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ},\mathbb{P}^{\circ})=0\leq\varepsilon_{n}
), we know that
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{TV}}(\theta)\geq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]
. Thus, the absolute value can be removed from the outer expression

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{TV}}(\theta)\;-\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{TV}}(\theta)\;-\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D_{\mathrm{TV}}\right)}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D_{\mathrm{TV}}\right)}\left(\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D_{\mathrm{TV}}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\ell(z;\theta)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\circ}}\bigl[\ell(z;\theta)\bigr]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon_{n}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};D_{\mathrm{TV}}\right)}\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}D_{\mathrm{TV}}\left(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\circ}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}\varepsilon_{n}.

Thus, for the Total Variation distance, the worst-case difference in expected loss is bounded by:

\Delta_{n}=\frac{K_{\ell}}{2}\varepsilon_{n}.

To achieve the desired
O(n^{-1})
rate for
\Delta_{n}
, we need to set the radius of the TV-ball as
\varepsilon_{n}\asymp n^{-1}
.
∎

Appendix I
Proof of "Fast Rate" DRO-DPO

I.1
Proof of "Fast Rate" WDPO

Proof.

From the "Master Theorem", all we need to do is verify that the Wasserstein DPO objective and the DPO loss function satisfy the two conditions.

1. Verification of Local Strong Convexity

From Appendix B.3, Lemma 11 of
Xu et al. 2025
, we know that
the Wasserstein DPO loss,
\mathcal{L}^{W}(\theta)
, is
\gamma\lambda
-strongly convex with respect to the Euclidean norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}
. This directly satisfies the first condition with a strong convexity parameter
\alpha=\gamma\lambda
where
\gamma=\frac{\beta^{2}e^{4\beta B}}{(1+e^{4\beta B})^{2}}
and
\lambda
are from the data coverage assumption.

2. Verification of Lipschitz Loss (in
\theta
) and
h_{\theta}
Linear In The Feature Map

We show that the pointwise DPO loss,
\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}(z;\theta)=-y\log\sigma(\beta h_{\theta})-(1-y)\log\sigma(-\beta h_{\theta})
, is Lipschitz in
\theta
. The gradient with respect to
\theta
is
\nabla_{\theta}\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}(z;\theta)=\partial\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}/\partial h_{\theta}\cdot\nabla_{\theta}h_{\theta}
.

First, we bound the norm of the gradient of the preference score. Using the log-linear policy assumption:

\displaystyle h_{\theta}(s,a^{1},a^{2})

\displaystyle:=\left(\log\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a^{1}|s)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(a^{1}|s)}\right)-\left(\log\frac{\pi_{\theta}(a^{2}|s)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(a^{2}|s)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\log\pi_{\theta}(a^{1}|s)-\log\pi_{\text{ref}}(a^{1}|s)\right)-\left(\log\pi_{\theta}(a^{2}|s)-\log\pi_{\text{ref}}(a^{2}|s)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\langle\theta,\psi(s,a^{1})\rangle-\langle\theta_{\text{ref}},\psi(s,a^{1})\rangle\right)-\left(\langle\theta,\psi(s,a^{2})\rangle-\langle\theta_{\text{ref}},\psi(s,a^{2})\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\langle\theta-\theta_{\text{ref}},\psi(s,a^{1})-\psi(s,a^{2})\rangle.

This is clearly a linear function in
\theta
. The gradient of
h_{\theta}
with respect to
\theta
is
\nabla_{\theta}h_{\theta}=\psi(s,a^{1})-\psi(s,a^{2})
. Its norm is bounded:

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla_{\theta}h_{\theta}\rVert_{2}=\lVert\psi(s,a^{1})-\psi(s,a^{2})\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\psi(s,a^{1})\rVert_{2}+\lVert\psi(s,a^{2})\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2.

Second, we bound the magnitude of the derivative of the logistic loss with respect to
h_{\theta}
.

\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}}{\partial h_{\theta}}=-y\beta(1-\sigma(\beta h_{\theta}))+(1-y)\beta\sigma(\beta h_{\theta})=\beta((1-y)\sigma(\beta h_{\theta})-y\sigma(-\beta h_{\theta})).

Since
y\in\{0,1\}
and
\sigma(\cdot)\in(0,1)
, the magnitude is maximized when either term is active, giving
\left|\partial\ell/\partial h_{\theta}\right|\leq\beta
. Combining these results, the norm of the gradient is bounded

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla_{\theta}\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}(z;\theta)\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\partial\ell}{\partial h_{\theta}}\right|\cdot\lVert\nabla_{\theta}h_{\theta}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta.

Thus, the pointwise loss of DPO is
L_{g}
-Lipschitz in
\theta
, with
L_{g}=2\beta
.

All four conditions of the Master Theorem have been verified for the Wasserstein DPO problem. We can now substitute the derived constants
\alpha=\gamma\lambda
,
L_{g}=2\beta
, and
\Delta_{n}=3\beta B
(from Appendix
5
) into the theorem’s final bound. This yields:

\lVert\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\mathcal{W}_{p}}-\theta^{\mathcal{W}_{p}}\rVert_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{2\beta^{2}}{\gamma^{2}\lambda^{2}}\log(1/\delta)+\frac{6\beta B}{\gamma\lambda}\right)}.

∎

I.2
Proof of "Fast Rate" KLDPO

Proof.

As we did for WDPO, we verify that the KL DPO objective satisfy the four conditions of the Master Theorem. We already proved that
\ell_{\mathrm{DPO}}
is Lipschitz in
\theta
and
h_{\theta}
is a linear function of the feature map, so we must just verify uniform boundedness and local strong convexity.

1. Verification of Local Strong Convexity

From Appendix C, Lemma 14 of
Xu et al. 2025
, the KL-DPO loss,
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{KL}}(\theta)
, is
\gamma\lambda
-strongly convex with respect to the Euclidean norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}
. This directly satisfies the first condition with a strong convexity parameter
\alpha=\gamma\lambda
where
\gamma=\frac{\beta^{2}e^{4\beta B}}{(1+e^{4\beta B})^{2}}
and
\lambda
is from the data coverage assumption.

2. Verification of Uniform Boundedness

From Appendix B.2, Lemma 9 of
Xu et al. 2025
, we know that the pointwise DPO loss is uniformly bounded by
\log(1+e^{4\beta B})
. This directly satisfies the conditions needed for Master Theorem.

Thus all four conditions of the Master Theorem have been verified for the KL-DPO problem. We can now substitute the derived constants
\alpha=\gamma\lambda
,
L_{g}=2\beta
,
K_{\ell}=\log\sigma(-4\beta B)
, and
\Delta_{n}=2^{-1/2}n^{-1}K_{\ell}
(from Appendix
H.2
) into the theorem’s final bound. This yields:

\lVert\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\mathrm{KL}}-\theta^{\mathrm{KL}}\rVert_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{2\beta^{2}}{\gamma^{2}\lambda^{2}}\log(1/\delta)+\frac{2\log(1+e^{4\beta B})}{\gamma\lambda}\right)}.

∎

Appendix J
Proofs for Sample Complexity of
\chi^{2}
-DPO

J.1
Proof of One-Step Performance Difference Lemma

Lemma 30

(One-Step Performance Difference Lemma)
.

Let
J(\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho,\;a\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)}\left[r_{*}(x,a)\right]
where
r_{*}\in\mathcal{F}
. Define the one-step baseline as
B^{\pi}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)}\left[r_{*}(x,a)\right]
and the one-step advantage as
A^{\pi}(x,a)=r_{*}(x,a)-B^{\pi}(x)
. Then we have the following one-step performance difference:

J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi^{\prime}}\left[A^{\pi}(x,a)\right].

Additionally, under the assumption that
V_{\max}(\pi):=\sup_{x,a}|A^{\pi}(x,a)|
, we also have

\left\lvert J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)\right\rvert\leq V_{\max}(\pi)\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\left[\lVert\pi^{\prime}\left(\cdot\mid x\right)-\pi\left(\cdot\mid x\right)\rVert_{1}\right].

Proof of Lemma
2
.

First notice that by definition

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)}\left[A^{\pi}(x,a)\right]=0.

(6)

Then notice the following:

\displaystyle J(\pi^{\prime})-J(\pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho,\;a\sim\pi^{\prime}(\cdot\mid x)}\left[r_{*}(x,a)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho,\;a\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)}\left[r_{*}(x,a)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho,\;a\sim\pi^{\prime}(\cdot\mid x)}\left[A^{\pi}(x,a)+B^{\pi}(x)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho,\;a\sim\pi(\cdot\mid x)}\left[A^{\pi}(x,a)+B^{\pi}(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi^{\prime}}\left[A^{\pi}(x,a)\right]

where the third equality holds from using Equation
6
in the second argument and the fact that
B^{\pi}(x)
depends only on
x
. To prove the second claim, assume for simplicity that the action space
\mathcal{A}
is countable. Then Equation
6
implies that
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}A^{\pi}(x,a)\pi(a\mid x)=0
. Using this, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi^{\prime}}\left[A^{\pi}(x,a)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\left[\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi^{\prime}}\left[A^{\pi}(x,a)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi}\left[A^{\pi}(x,a)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\left[\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}A^{\pi}(x,a)\left(\pi^{\prime}\left(a\mid x\right)-\pi\left(a\mid x\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq V_{\max}(\pi)\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho}\left[\lVert\pi^{\prime}\left(\cdot\mid x\right)-\pi\left(\cdot\mid x\right)\rVert_{1}\right]

where the last inequality holds from Hölder’s inequality. It should be noted that since
r_{*}\in\mathcal{F}
, there exist some
\omega^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such as
r_{*}(x,a)=\phi(x,a)^{\top}\omega^{*}
. Thus, one can bound the one-step advantage as
\sup_{x,a}\left\lvert A^{\pi}(x,a)\right\rvert\leq 2F
so we can take
V_{\max}(\pi)=2F
.
∎

J.2
Proof of Log-Linear Policy Lipschitzness

Lemma 31

(Log-Linear Policies are Lipschitz)
.

Let
\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)\propto\exp\{\theta^{\top}\psi(x,\cdot)\}
and assume that

\sup_{x,a}\|\psi(x,a)\|_{2}\leq B
. Then for every fixed
x
,

\bigl\|\pi_{\theta^{\prime}}(\cdot\mid x)-\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)\bigr\|_{1}\;\leq\;2B\,\|\theta^{\prime}-\theta\|_{2}.

In particular, if
B=1
(Assumption
2
), the map is
2
-Lipschitz.

Proof of Lemma
3
.

Fix
x
and define
f(\theta)=\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}|}
.
By the fundamental theorem of calculus along the line segment
\theta_{t}=\theta+t(\theta^{\prime}-\theta)
,

f(\theta^{\prime})-f(\theta)=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla f(\theta_{t})\,(\theta^{\prime}-\theta)\,dt,

hence

\|f(\theta^{\prime})-f(\theta)\|_{1}\leq\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\nabla f(\theta_{t})\|_{2\to 1}\Big)\,\|\theta^{\prime}-\theta\|_{2}.

For each
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

\nabla_{\theta}\pi_{\theta}(a\mid x)=\pi_{\theta}(a\mid x)\Big(\psi(x,a)-\mu_{\theta}(x)\Big),\qquad\mu_{\theta}(x):=\mathbb{E}_{a^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}[\psi(x,a^{\prime})].

Thus for any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, assuming
\mathcal{A}
is countable

\displaystyle\bigl\|\nabla f(\theta)\,u\bigr\|_{1}=\sum_{a}\big|\langle\nabla_{\theta}\pi_{\theta}(a\mid x),u\rangle\big|\;

\displaystyle\leq\;\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\theta}(a\mid x)\,\|\psi(x,a)-\mu_{\theta}(x)\|_{2}\,\|u\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\theta}(a\mid x)\big(\|\psi(x,a)\|_{2}+\|\mu_{\theta}(x)\|_{2}\big)\Big)\,\|u\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\left[\|\psi(x,\cdot)\|_{2}+\|\mu_{\theta}(x)\|_{2}\right]\Big)\,\|u\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2B\,\|u\|_{2},

where we used
\|\mu_{\theta}(x)\|_{2}\leq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\theta}}\|\psi(x,\cdot)\|_{2}\leq B
by Jensen’s inequality.
Therefore,
\|\nabla f(\theta)\|_{2\to 1}\leq 2B
uniformly in
\theta
, and the claim follows.
∎

J.3
Proof of Sample Complexity Performance Gap of
\chi^{2}
-DPO

Theorem 15

(Sample Complexity Result for
\chi^{2}
-DPO)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Let

\theta^{*}:=\arg\min_{\theta}\mathcal{L}^{D}(\theta;\varepsilon_{n})
,
\hat{\theta}_{n}:=\arg\min_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{n}^{D}(\theta;\varepsilon_{n})
its empirical minimizer.
Then for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\forall\,\theta\in\Theta,\pi\in\Pi:\quad|J_{\mathrm{rob}}(\pi_{\theta})-J_{\mathrm{rob}}(\hat{\pi})|\;\leq\;2\,V_{\max}(\hat{\pi})\Big(\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}+E_{n}(\delta)\Big),

where
V_{\max}(\pi):=\sup_{x,a}|A^{\pi}(x,a)|\leq 2F
and, for
\chi^{2}
/KL–DPO,

E_{n}(\delta)\;\lesssim\;\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\!\left(\frac{2\beta^{2}}{\gamma^{2}\lambda^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\;+\;\frac{\log(1+e^{4\beta B})}{\gamma\lambda}\right)}.

Consequently,

\forall\,\theta\in\Theta,\pi\in\Pi:\quad|J_{\mathrm{rob}}(\hat{\pi})-J_{\mathrm{rob}}(\pi^{*})|\;\lesssim\;2V_{\mathrm{max}}(\hat{\pi})\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\!\left(\frac{2\beta^{2}}{\gamma^{2}\lambda^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\frac{\log(1+e^{4\beta B})}{\gamma\lambda}\right)}.

Proof of Theorem
9
.

First note the following:

\displaystyle\left\lvert J_{\mathrm{rob}}(\pi_{\theta})-J_{\mathrm{rob}}(\hat{\pi})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2})}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho_{\mathbb{Q}},\;a\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\left[r_{*}(x,a)\right]-\inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2})}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho_{\mathbb{Q}},\;a\sim\hat{\pi}(\cdot\mid x)}\left[r_{*}(x,a)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\sup_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2})}\left(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho_{\mathbb{Q}},\;a\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\left[r_{*}(x,a)\right]-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho_{\mathbb{Q}},\;a\sim\hat{\pi}(\cdot\mid x)}\left[r_{*}(x,a)\right]\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\sup_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2})}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi_{\theta}}\left[A^{\hat{\pi}}(x,a)\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq V_{\mathrm{max}}(\hat{\pi})\left\lvert\sup_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{P}^{\circ};\chi^{2})}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}}\left[\lVert\pi_{\theta}\left(\cdot\mid x\right)-\hat{\pi}\left(\cdot\mid x\right)\rVert_{1}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2V_{\mathrm{max}}(\hat{\pi})\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{n}\|_{2},

where the first inequality follows from
\inf_{x}f(x)-\inf_{x}g(x)\leq\sup_{x}|f(x)-g(x)|
, the second inequality holds from Lemma
2
, and the last inequality holds from Lemma
3
. Define
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}=\left\{\lVert\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{*}\rVert_{2}\leq E_{n}(\delta)\right\}
. By the Master Theorem (Theorem
4
), we have that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\delta})\geq 1-\delta
. Thus conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}
and by triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert J_{\mathrm{rob}}(\pi_{\theta})-J_{\mathrm{rob}}(\pi^{*})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2V_{\mathrm{max}}(\hat{\pi})\left(\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}+\|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2V_{\mathrm{max}}(\hat{\pi})\left(\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}+E_{n}(\delta)\right).

Thus taking
\theta=\theta^{*}
, we conclude with probability atleast
1-\delta

\forall\,\theta\in\Theta,\pi\in\Pi:\quad|J_{\mathrm{rob}}(\hat{\pi})-J_{\mathrm{rob}}(\pi^{*})|\;\lesssim\;2V_{\mathrm{max}}(\hat{\pi})\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\!\left(\frac{2\beta^{2}}{\gamma^{2}\lambda^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\frac{\log(1+e^{4\beta B})}{\gamma\lambda}\right)}.

∎

Appendix K
Proof of Tractable
\chi^{2}
-DRO-REBEL

Proof of
2
.

The proof that follows is standard in the analysis of f-divergences and follows from
Namkoong and Duchi 2017b
. We include it for completeness. Let
\mathbb{P}_{n}
be the empirical distribution. The robust optimization problem is given by

\mathcal{L}^{\chi^{2}}_{n}(\theta;\rho)=\sup_{\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\ell(z;\theta)]\quad\text{s.t.}\quad D_{\chi^{2}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{P}_{n})\leq\rho,\quad\mathbb{P}\geq 0,\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[1]=1.

The
\chi^{2}
-divergence is defined by
f(t)=\frac{1}{2}(t-1)^{2}
. The Fenchel conjugate
f^{*}(s)=\sup_{t\geq 0}\{st-f(t)\}
.
For
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
f^{\prime}(t)=t-1
. Setting
s=t-1
, we get
t=s+1
.
Substituting this into the definition of
f^{*}(s)
,

f^{*}(s)=s(s+1)-\frac{1}{2}((s+1)-1)^{2}=s^{2}+s-\frac{1}{2}s^{2}=\frac{1}{2}s^{2}+s
.
This derivation holds for
t\geq 0
, which implies
s+1\geq 0\implies s\geq-1
. If
s<-1
, the optimal
t
would be negative, violating
t\geq 0
. In this case,
f^{*}(s)
becomes
\infty
due to the constraint
t\geq 0
. According to Lemma
10

[
Duchi and Namkoong 2020
]
, the dual form of the
f
